This imbalance extends into the private sector,\nwhere an entrenched culture of seniority have put the better jobs out of reach for the country’s\nyoung. Italy has always suffered under a hierarchical system, with the young deferring to authority\nuntil it’s their time to take the reins. “You are not considered experienced based on your CV, on your\nability or according to your skills, but just based on your age,” says Federico Soldani, 37, an\nepidemiologist who left Pisa and now works in Washington, D.C. “When you are under 40, you are\nconsidered young.” The system worked — to a certain extent — as long as the economy was growing.\nPatience paid off as jobs opened to whoever was next in line. But with the extended slump, the labour\nmarket has seized up. “The queue is not moving forward anymore”. "必须将‘心理状态的显性陈述与隐性行为的一致性’作为独立核验维度，严禁仅凭是否存在礼貌用语或语气强弱来单一判定态度是否符合，需同时验证模型是否通过直接心理描写或具体行为逻辑准确呈现了目标状态。", "application_guide": "Generator 在设计 Rubric 时，针对态度类要求应设立两条互补准则：1. ‘显性确认’：检查故事中是否有角色直接陈述其心理状态（如‘我不感到抱歉’、‘我坚持要答案’）；2. ‘行为逻辑一致性’：检查角色的对话和行为是否排除了与该态度相悖的要素（如‘不感到抱歉’则不应出现‘希望你不介意’等道歉缓冲语，‘坚决’则不应表现出犹豫或退缩）。只有当两者结合评估，确认角色既表达了该态度且在行为上未表现出相反倾向时，才判定为达标。避免仅因使用了礼貌词汇就判定为‘感到抱歉’，也避免仅因没有直接说‘我不抱歉’而忽略其坚定的行为表现。", "retrieval_source": "semantic", "historical_reward": null, "evidence_tasks": 0}, {"experience_id": "6", "trigger": "当任务涉及虚构设定（如魔法、超自然生物、特殊生理状态）且包含明确的逻辑约束（如‘未出生’、‘不可见’、‘无形’）时", "principle": "必须将‘核心设定逻辑的自洽性’作为最高优先级的否决项，严禁将‘表面动作描写的详尽程度’置于‘角色行为是否符合设定前提’之上；若模型为追求描写生动而违反核心设定（如让未出生婴儿物理在场），即使描写细腻也应判为不合格。", "application_guide": "Generator 在设计 Rubric 时，针对奇幻或特殊设定任务：1. 首先设立一条‘设定逻辑核验’准则，明确列出任务中不可违反的物理或逻辑边界（如‘未出生角色只能通过心灵/声音互动，不得物理移动’）；2. 将该准则设为‘一票否决’或极高权重，一旦违反直接判定低分；3. 将‘动作细节描写’降级为次要加分项，仅在逻辑完全自洽的前提下评估。避免仅因缺少某段具体的‘起床动作’描写而给一个逻辑正确但开篇紧凑的回答打低分，同时给一个逻辑崩坏但描写详细的回答打高分。", "retrieval_source": "semantic", "historical_reward": null, "evidence_tasks": 0}, {"experience_id": "7", "trigger": "当任务要求生成内容长度严格限定为'only N characters/words'时", "principle": "必须将'only N'的语义明确定义为'精确等于N'还是'不超过N'，避免默认假设为上限导致与用户真实意图（特别是需要精确长度时）不符，或因默认假设为精确值导致对轻微超出的过度惩罚。", "application_guide": "Generator在设计评分标准时，若遇到'only N'的长度约束，不应直接使用模糊的'不超过'或'等于'，而应结合上下文判断：若上下文暗示是最大容量（如'limit', 'max'），则设为'不超过'；若上下文强调精确规格（如'exact length', 'precise count'），则设为'精确等于'。若上下文不明，应在标准中明确写出'精确等于N'或'不超过N'，并保留注释说明选择依据，防止因术语歧义导致评分失效。", "retrieval_source": "semantic", "historical_reward": null, "application_guide": "Generator 在设计评分标准时，若原文包含明确的实体清单（如'Vietnam, Singapore...'），对应的评分准则必须明确要求‘列出原文提及的具体实体名称’或‘完整覆盖原文列举的实体集合’。不能仅设定为‘提及该群体’或‘提及部分代表’。在应用时，若模型仅使用上位词（如'Asian countries'）而未复现具体名称，应直接判定为‘内容遗漏’并扣分，防止因‘大意正确’而掩盖‘事实细节缺失’。", "retrieval_source": "semantic", "historical_reward": null, "evidence_tasks": 0}, {"experience_id": "9", "trigger": "Generator 在设计 Rubric 时，针对目标导向类任务，应设立一条专门核验‘逻辑自洽性’的标准：提问‘故事中角色的关键行动（如寻找物品、对话决策）是否与预设的目标逻辑一致？是否存在为了推进剧情而违背常识或角色动机的矛盾行为（如将武器交给敌人以击败敌人）？’。若发现此类逻辑断裂，无论结局如何描述，均应判定该标准不达标。此标准应优先于单纯的‘结局呈现’标准，防止模型通过荒谬逻辑强行拼凑结局。", "retrieval_source": "semantic", "historical_reward": null, "evidence_tasks": 0}, {"experience_id": "10", "trigger": But this time, instead\\nof peasant farmers and manual labourers taking steamships bound for New York City, Italy is losing its best\\nand brightest to a decade of economic stagnation, a frozen labour market and an entrenched system of\\npatronage and nepotism.\\n\\nTake Luca Vigliero, a 31-year-old architect. After graduating from the University of Genoa and failing to find\\nsatisfying work at home, he moved abroad, working first for a year for Metropolitan Architecture in Rotterdam\\nand then accepting a job in Dubai. In Italy, his résumé had drawn no interest. At Dubai’s X Architects, he was\\nquickly promoted. Escape from Italy has also allowed Vigliero to fast-track his life plans. He and his wife\\nhad a son in September; had they remained in Italy, he says they would not have been able to afford children\\nthis soon. “All my friends in Italy are not married, they have really basic work, they live with their parents,”\\nhe says. “Here, there’s a future. In Italy everything is stopped.” Italy doesn’t keep track of how many of its\\nyoung professionals are seeking their fortunes abroad, but there’s plenty of anecdotal evidence that the number\\nis rising. The number of Italians ages 25 to 39 with degrees registering with the national government as living\\nabroad every year rose steadily, from 2,540 in 2016 to about 4,000 in 2020. The research-institute Censis\\nestimated that 11,700 graduates found work abroad in 2018 — that’s one out of every 25 Italians who graduated\\nthat year. According to a poll by a Milanese recruitment agency, 33.6% of new graduates felt they needed to\\nleave the country to take advantage of their education. A year later, 61.5% feel that they should have done\\nso. It’s not hard to see why. Italy’s economic woes have fallen hard on the shoulders of the country’s youth.\\nAccording to OECD figures for 2020, just over 8 out of every 10 Italians aged between 15 and 29 years-old\\nstill lived at home with their parents. One in 5 young people ages 15 to 29 had basically dropped out: not\\nstudying, not training, not working. “We’re condemning an entire generation into a black hole,” says Celli.\\n\\n\\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\\nfinding work that fits their qualifications. The unemployment rate among Italian graduates is 7% and\\n14% for graduates between the ages 25 to 29, more than double the rate in the rest of Europe and\\nmuch higher than that of their less-educated peers. Italians have a word for the problem:\\ngerontocracy, or rule by the elderly. Too much of the economy is geared towards looking after older\\nItalians. While the country spends relatively little on housing, unemployment and child care —\\nexpenditures the young depend upon to launch their careers — it has maintained some of the highest\\npensions in Europe, in part by increasing borrowing. This imbalance extends into the private sector,\\nwhere an entrenched culture of seniority have put the better jobs out of reach for the country’s\\nyoung. Italy has always suffered under a hierarchical system, with the young deferring to authority\\nuntil it’s their time to take the reins. “You are not considered experienced based on your CV, on your\\nability or according to your skills, but just based on your age,” says Federico Soldani, 37, an\\nepidemiologist who left Pisa and now works in Washington, D.C. “When you are under 40, you are\\nconsidered young.” The system worked — to a certain extent — as long as the economy was growing.\\nPatience paid off as jobs opened to whoever was next in line. But with the extended slump, the labour\\nmarket has seized up. “The queue is not moving forward anymore”. Entry to some professions —\\nlike the lucrative position of public notary — is so limited that the job has become all but hereditary.\\nIn a country where success is built on relationships and seniority, only the friends and children of the\\nelite have a chance to jump the queue.\\n\\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\\nfinding work that fits their qualifications. Choose to go where they still value loyalty, respect and the recognition of merit and results.”\nThe letter, published in Italy’s La Repubblica newspaper created a great debate in Italy. Celli, many agreed,\nhad articulated a growing sense in his son’s generation that the best hopes for success lie abroad. Commentators\npoint to an accelerating exodus of young Italians and worry that the country is losing its most valuable resource.\nAnd with reforms made all but impossible by Italy’s deep-rooted interests and erratic politics, many are\nstarting to wonder if the trend can be reversed. “We have a flow outward and almost no flow inward,” says\nSergio Nava, author of the book and blog The Flight of Talent. The motives of those leaving haven’t changed\nmuch since the last wave of economic migrants left to make their fortunes a century ago. But this time, instead\nof peasant farmers and manual labourers taking steamships bound for New York City, Italy is losing its best\nand brightest to a decade of economic stagnation, a frozen labour market and an entrenched system of\npatronage and nepotism.\n\nTake Luca Vigliero, a 31-year-old architect. After graduating from the University of Genoa and failing to find\nsatisfying work at home, he moved abroad, working first for a year for Metropolitan Architecture in Rotterdam\nand then accepting a job in Dubai. In Italy, his résumé had drawn no interest. At Dubai’s X Architects, he was\nquickly promoted. Escape from Italy has also allowed Vigliero to fast-track his life plans. He and his wife\nhad a son in September; had they remained in Italy, he says they would not have been able to afford children\nthis soon. “All my friends in Italy are not married, they have really basic work, they live with their parents,”\nhe says. “Here, there’s a future. In Italy everything is stopped.” Italy doesn’t keep track of how many of its\nyoung professionals are seeking their fortunes abroad, but there’s plenty of anecdotal evidence that the number\nis rising. The number of Italians ages 25 to 39 with degrees registering with the national government as living\nabroad every year rose steadily, from 2,540 in 2016 to about 4,000 in 2020. The research-institute Censis\nestimated that 11,700 graduates found work abroad in 2018 — that’s one out of every 25 Italians who graduated\nthat year. According to a poll by a Milanese recruitment agency, 33.6% of new graduates felt they needed to\nleave the country to take advantage of their education. A year later, 61.5% feel that they should have done\nso. It’s not hard to see why. Italy’s economic woes have fallen hard on the shoulders of the country’s youth.\nAccording to OECD figures for 2020, just over 8 out of every 10 Italians aged between 15 and 29 years-old\nstill lived at home with their parents. One in 5 young people ages 15 to 29 had basically dropped out: not\nstudying, not training, not working. “We’re condemning an entire generation into a black hole,” says Celli.\n\n\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\nfinding work that fits their qualifications. The unemployment rate among Italian graduates is 7% and\n14% for graduates between the ages 25 to 29, more than double the rate in the rest of Europe and\nmuch higher than that of their less-educated peers. Italians have a word for the problem:\ngerontocracy, or rule by the elderly. Too much of the economy is geared towards looking after older\nItalians. While the country spends relatively little on housing, unemployment and child care —\nexpenditures the young depend upon to launch their careers — it has maintained some of the highest\npensions in Europe, in part by increasing borrowing. This imbalance extends into the private sector,\nwhere an entrenched culture of seniority have put the better jobs out of reach for the country’s\nyoung. Italy has always suffered under a hierarchical system, with the young deferring to authority\nuntil it’s their time to take the reins. “You are not considered experienced based on your CV, on your\nability or according to your skills, but just based on your age,” says Federico Soldani, 37, an\nepidemiologist who left Pisa and now works in Washington, D.C. “When you are under 40, you are\nconsidered young.” The system worked — to a certain extent — as long as the economy was growing.\nPatience paid off as jobs opened to whoever was next in line.
